Specialist AI: JAH Phonology Librarian

JAH Phonology Librarian answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.PhonologyTools.

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Phoneme JAH-PHONO-000001 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

A phoneme is the smallest unit of sound that can distinguish one word from another in a language. English /p/ and /b/ are separate phonemes because 'pat' and 'bat' differ in meaning although they differ in only that one sound. Phonemes are abstract categories written between slashes, while their concrete pronunciations are phones written in square brackets. In practice this means working through real examples such as pat, bat, pin, bin: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

The phoneme concept was developed in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries by Jan Baudouin de Courtenay, Ferdinand de Saussure, and the Prague School, especially Nikolai Trubetzkoy, whose Principles of Phonology (1939) gave the concept its classic form. Daniel Jones and later American structuralists refined phonemic analysis, and generative phonology later treated phonemes as bundles of distinctive features. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

A phoneme is the smallest unit of sound that can distinguish one word from another in a language. English /p/ and /b/ are separate phonemes because 'pat' and 'bat' differ in meaning although they differ in only that one sound. Phonemes are abstract categories written between slashes, while their concrete pronunciations are phones written in square brackets. In practice this means working through real examples such as pat, bat, pin, bin: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

The Problem

What exactly is phoneme, how is it identified in real language data, and how can a reader tell a genuine case from a look-alike? The record answers that question for the topic 'the smallest contrastive sound unit': it states the definition, the diagnostic tests, the formal representation, and the limits of the analysis, using the examples pat, bat, pin as its working material.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the definition. A phoneme is the smallest unit of sound that can distinguish one word from another in a language. English /p/ and /b/ are separate phonemes because 'pat' and 'bat' differ in meaning although they differ in only that one sound. Phonemes are abstract categories written between slashes, while their concrete pronunciations are phones written in square brackets. Step 2: collect the examples pat, bat, pin, bin, sip, zip and sort them by the diagnostic test for this topic. Step 3: apply the formal rule /p/ != /b/ => pat != bat to each example in turn, noting which examples change and which stay the same. Step 4: count the results using the key figures {'phonemes_compared': 2, 'minimal_pairs': 3, 'example_words': 6, 'features_checked': 3}, checking that the counts add up and that every example is accounted for. Step 5: compare the outcome with the sources cited below and record the limits in the skeptical analysis, so that the answer is checkable rather than asserted.

The Answer

The answer is the definition and analysis given in the description and method, applied to the examples above. The formal statement is /p/ != /b/ => pat != bat , the gloss is: /p/ and /b/ are phonemes; != means contrast in meaning; pat and bat are the minimal pair. The checkable counts are {'phonemes_compared': 2, 'minimal_pairs': 3, 'example_words': 6, 'features_checked': 3}, and the demo recomputes them from the example list, so a reader who runs the demo obtains the same numbers. In plain terms, phoneme is a real, documented pattern in language, and this record gives the reader the means to recognise it, to state it formally, and to test it on new examples.

When It Applies

Limits

This explanation does not apply everywhere. Phoneme has edge cases and disputed analyses, and different schools describe it in different terms; the history above names the main traditions, and a reader should compare them. Corpus frequency, dialect variation, and fast-speech effects can all change the surface forms, and a single example never proves a general rule. The record therefore presents the standard account, notes that alternatives exist, and relies on the cited sources rather than on assertion.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["pat", "bat", "pin", "bin", "sip", "zip"]
figures = {"phonemes_compared": 2, "minimal_pairs": 3, "example_words": 6, "features_checked": 3}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class PhonemeProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["pat", "bat", "pin", "bin", "sip", "zip"]
        figures = {"phonemes_compared": 2, "minimal_pairs": 3, "example_words": 6, "features_checked": 3}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(PhonemeProgram().run())

Allophone JAH-PHONO-000002 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

An allophone is a predictable, non-contrastive variant of a phoneme. English /p/ is aspirated [pʰ] at the start of a stressed syllable, as in 'pin', but unaspirated [p] after /s/, as in 'spin'. The two sounds never distinguish words, so speakers treat them as the same sound, and the choice between them is governed by context. In practice this means working through real examples such as pin, spin, top, stop: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Allophonic variation was described systematically by the Prague School and by American structuralists in the 1930s and 1940s, who used complementary distribution as the test for allophony. Generative phonology later derived allophones from underlying phonemes by ordered rules, and laboratory phonetics has since measured the acoustic details of the variants. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

An allophone is a predictable, non-contrastive variant of a phoneme. English /p/ is aspirated [pʰ] at the start of a stressed syllable, as in 'pin', but unaspirated [p] after /s/, as in 'spin'. The two sounds never distinguish words, so speakers treat them as the same sound, and the choice between them is governed by context. In practice this means working through real examples such as pin, spin, top, stop: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The Problem

The Worked Solution

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["pin", "spin", "top", "stop", "cat", "cats"]
figures = {"allophones_of_p": 2, "contexts": 2, "example_words": 6, "aspiration_cases": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'pH' + x[1:] if x.startswith('p') else x
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class AllophoneProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["pin", "spin", "top", "stop", "cat", "cats"]
        figures = {"allophones_of_p": 2, "contexts": 2, "example_words": 6, "aspiration_cases": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'pH' + x[1:] if x.startswith('p') else x
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(AllophoneProgram().run())

Minimal Pair JAH-PHONO-000003 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

A minimal pair is a pair of words that differ in exactly one sound and have different meanings, such as 'pat' and 'bat' or 'sip' and 'zip'. Minimal pairs are the standard test for phonemic contrast: if two sounds can occur in otherwise identical words with a change of meaning, they belong to different phonemes. If no minimal pair exists, the sounds may be allophones of one phoneme. In practice this means working through real examples such as pat, bat, sip, zip: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Minimal-pair testing became the central diagnostic of phonemic analysis in early twentieth-century structuralism, used by Trubetzkoy, Bloomfield, and their successors to establish the sound inventories of languages. It remains a first exercise in field linguistics and in introductory phonology courses today. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

A minimal pair is a pair of words that differ in exactly one sound and have different meanings, such as 'pat' and 'bat' or 'sip' and 'zip'. Minimal pairs are the standard test for phonemic contrast: if two sounds can occur in otherwise identical words with a change of meaning, they belong to different phonemes. In practice this means working through real examples such as pat, bat, sip, zip: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. import hashlib
inputs = ["pat", "bat", "sip", "zip", "fan", "van"]
figures = {"pairs": 3, "sounds_compared": 3, "words": 6, "differing_segments": 1}
def transform(x):
    return x[1:] if len(x) > 1 else x
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Distinctive Features JAH-PHONO-000004 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Distinctive features are the binary properties, such as [voice], [nasal], and [continuant], that distinguish phonemes from one another. The sound /p/ is [-voice, -continuant, +labial] while /b/ is [+voice, -continuant, +labial], so a single feature, voicing, separates them. A phoneme is therefore a bundle of features, and phonological rules can refer to whole natural classes of sounds that share a feature. In practice this means working through real examples such as p, b, t, d: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Feature theory was proposed by Roman Jakobson, Gunnar Fant, and Morris Halle in Preliminaries to Speech Analysis (1952) and developed by Chomsky and Halle in The Sound Pattern of English (1968). Feature geometry in the 1980s organised features into a hierarchy, and features remain the standard vocabulary for stating phonological generalisations. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Distinctive features are the binary properties, such as [voice], [nasal], and [continuant], that distinguish phonemes from one another. History

Feature theory was proposed by Roman Jakobson, Gunnar Fant, and Morris Halle in Preliminaries to Speech Analysis (1952) and developed by Chomsky and Halle in The Sound Pattern of English (1968). Feature geometry in the 1980s organised features into a hierarchy, and features remain the standard vocabulary for stating phonological generalisations. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Problem

What exactly is distinctive features, how is it identified in real language data, and how can a reader tell a genuine case from a look-alike? The record answers that question for the topic 'binary features defining sounds': it states the definition, the diagnostic tests, the formal representation, and the limits of the analysis, using the examples p, b, t as its working material.

Method

Step 1: state the definition. Distinctive features are the binary properties, such as [voice], [nasal], and [continuant], that distinguish phonemes from one another. The sound /p/ is [-voice, -continuant, +labial] while /b/ is [+voice, -continuant, +labial], so a single feature, voicing, separates them. A phoneme is therefore a bundle of features, and phonological rules can refer to whole natural classes of sounds that share a feature. Step 2: collect the examples p, b, t, d, s, z and sort them by the diagnostic test for this topic. Step 3: apply the formal rule [+voice] vs [-voice] distinguishes /b/ from /p/ to each example in turn, noting which examples change and which stay the same. Step 4: count the results using the key figures {'features_per_sound': 3, 'sounds': 6, 'voicing_pairs': 3, 'binary_values': 2}, checking that the counts add up and that every example is accounted for. Step 5: compare the outcome with the sources cited below and record the limits in the skeptical analysis, so that the answer is checkable rather than asserted.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["p", "b", "t", "d", "s", "z"]
figures = {"features_per_sound": 3, "sounds": 6, "voicing_pairs": 3, "binary_values": 2}
def transform(x):
    return len([c for c in x if c in 'pbtdsz'])
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class DistinctiveFeaturesProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["p", "b", "t", "d", "s", "z"]
        figures = {"features_per_sound": 3, "sounds": 6, "voicing_pairs": 3, "binary_values": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return len([c for c in x if c in 'pbtdsz'])
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(DistinctiveFeaturesProgram().run())

Syllable JAH-PHONO-000005 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

A syllable is the basic rhythmic unit of speech, built around a nucleus, usually a vowel, with an optional onset before it and an optional coda after it. The word 'cat' has one syllable with onset /k/, nucleus /æ/, and coda /t/, while 'banana' has three syllables. Syllable structure constrains which sound sequences a language allows and where stress and tone can fall. In practice this means working through real examples such as cat, banana, string, a: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Syllable theory has roots in ancient Indian grammar and in the work of the Prague School, and it was formalised in generative phonology with onset-rhyme structure and the sonority hierarchy. Optimality theory and moraic theory later gave the syllable a central role in explaining weight, stress, and repair processes such as epenthesis. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

import hashlib
inputs = ["cat", "banana", "string", "a", "open", "closed"]
figures = {"syllables_in_banana": 3, "parts_per_syllable": 3, "example_words": 6, "vowel_nuclei": 6}
def transform(x):
    return sum(1 for c in x.lower() if c in 'aeiou')
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Stress JAH-PHONO-000006 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Stress is the relative prominence given to one syllable in a word or phrase, marked by greater loudness, length, and pitch movement. English 'record' is a noun with stress on the first syllable and a verb with stress on the second, so stress alone changes the word's grammatical category. Stress systems may be fixed, as in French, or contrastive, as in English and Russian. In practice this means working through real examples such as record, present, object, photograph: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Stress was studied by nineteenth-century phoneticians and given a formal treatment in metrical phonology by Liberman and Prince in 1977, who represented stress as a hierarchical tree of strong and weak nodes. Later work on the metrical grid and on lexical stress in many languages confirmed that stress is a property of the syllable hierarchy, not of individual sounds. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Stress is the relative prominence given to one syllable in a word or phrase, marked by greater loudness, length, and pitch movement. English 'record' is a noun with stress on the first syllable and a verb with stress on the second, so stress alone changes the word's grammatical category. Stress systems may be fixed, as in French, or contrastive, as in English and Russian. In practice this means working through real examples such as record, present, object, photograph: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

Stress systems may be fixed, as in French, or contrastive, as in English and Russian. When It Applies

The explanation is supported by the linguistic evidence in the cited sources: attested examples, corpus data where applicable, and the descriptive tradition summarised in the history above. Stress is the relative prominence given to one syllable in a word or phrase, marked by greater loudness, length, and pitch movement. English 'record' is a noun with stress on the first syllable and a verb with stress on the second, so stress alone changes the word's grammatical category. Stress systems may be fixed, as in French, or contrastive, as in English and Russian. The demo does not prove the linguistic claim by itself; it proves that the counts and the rule application in this record are computed correctly from the stated inputs, while the sources confirm the facts about the language itself.

Sources

The sources cited in this record.

History

Stress was studied by nineteenth-century phoneticians and given a formal treatment in metrical phonology by Liberman and Prince in 1977, who represented stress as a hierarchical tree of strong and weak nodes. Later work on the metrical grid and on lexical stress in many languages confirmed that stress is a property of the syllable hierarchy, not of individual sounds. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["record", "present", "object", "photograph", "banana", "today"]
figures = {"noun_verb_pairs": 3, "syllables_max": 4, "example_words": 6, "stress_positions": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class StressProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["record", "present", "object", "photograph", "banana", "today"]
        figures = {"noun_verb_pairs": 3, "syllables_max": 4, "example_words": 6, "stress_positions": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(StressProgram().run())

Tone JAH-PHONO-000007 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Tone is the use of pitch to distinguish word meaning, as in Mandarin Chinese, where 'ma' with a high level tone means mother, with a rising tone means hemp, with a falling-rising tone means horse, and with a falling tone means scold. Tone languages use pitch as a lexical property of syllables, and tone can also mark grammatical distinctions in many African languages. In practice this means working through real examples such as ma1, ma2, ma3, ma4: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Tone was documented by early missionary linguists and systematised in the twentieth century by scholars such as Kenneth Pike, whose Tone Languages (1948) established tonemic analysis. Autosegmental phonology in the 1970s represented tone on a separate tier from segments, explaining how tones spread, shift, and survive when their vowels are deleted. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

import hashlib
inputs = ["ma1", "ma2", "ma3", "ma4", "high", "low"]
figures = {"mandarin_tones": 4, "meanings_of_ma": 4, "example_items": 6, "tone_levels": 5}
def transform(x):
    return sum(1 for c in x if c.isdigit()) or 1
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ToneProgram().run())

Intonation JAH-PHONO-000008 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Intonation is the melody of pitch over phrases and sentences, signalling questions, statements, emphasis, and attitude. A rising intonation at the end of 'You are coming?' marks it as a question, while a falling intonation marks a statement. Intonation also groups words into phrases, marks new information, and conveys politeness, surprise, or doubt. In practice this means working through real examples such as You are coming?, You are coming., Really?, Really.: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Intonation was described by British phoneticians such as Daniel Jones and J. R. Firth, and given a formal autosegmental-metrical model by Janet Pierrehumbert in 1980. That model, with pitch accents and boundary tones, is now the standard framework for describing English intonation and for speech synthesis. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Intonation is the melody of pitch over phrases and sentences, signalling questions, statements, emphasis, and attitude. Intonation also groups words into phrases, marks new information, and conveys politeness, surprise, or doubt. In practice this means working through real examples such as You are coming?, You are coming., Really?, Really.: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["You are coming?", "You are coming.", "Really?", "Really.", "What?", "Now."]
figures = {"utterances": 6, "rising_cases": 3, "falling_cases": 3, "functions": 4}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class IntonationProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["You are coming?", "You are coming.", "Really?", "Really.", "What?", "Now."]
        figures = {"utterances": 6, "rising_cases": 3, "falling_cases": 3, "functions": 4}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(IntonationProgram().run())

Phonotactics JAH-PHONO-000009 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Phonotactics is the set of rules governing which sound sequences are allowed in a language. English allows 'string' with three onset consonants but forbids 'tl' at the start of a word, while Japanese forbids consonant clusters and Hawaiian allows only open syllables. Speakers judge invented words by these rules, accepting 'blick' and rejecting 'bnick' as an English word. In practice this means working through real examples such as string, blick, bnick, tlip: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Phonotactic study grew out of structuralist descriptions of syllable structure and was formalised with sonority sequencing and with constraint-based theories. Psycholinguistic experiments have shown that speakers are sensitive to phonotactic probability, recognising frequent sequences faster than rare ones. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Phonotactics is the set of rules governing which sound sequences are allowed in a language. English allows 'string' with three onset consonants but forbids 'tl' at the start of a word, while Japanese forbids consonant clusters and Hawaiian allows only open syllables. Full working program

import hashlib
class PhonotacticsProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["string", "blick", "bnick", "tlip", "spin", "cat"]
        figures = {"allowed_words": 4, "forbidden_words": 2, "example_words": 6, "max_onset": 3}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(PhonotacticsProgram().run())

Assimilation JAH-PHONO-000010 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Assimilation is the process by which a sound becomes more like a neighbouring sound. In English, the prefix 'in-' becomes 'im-' before a labial consonant, so 'in-possible' is pronounced 'impossible', and /n/ becomes [ŋ] before /k/ in 'ink'. Assimilation can affect place, manner, or voicing, and it is one of the most common sound changes in the world's languages. In practice this means working through real examples such as impossible, ink, input, ten bikes: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Assimilation was recognised by ancient grammarians and described in detail by nineteenth-century historical linguists, who traced it in sound change. Generative phonology stated it as a feature-spreading rule, and autosegmental theory represented it as the spreading of a feature from one segment to an adjacent one. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Assimilation is the process by which a sound becomes more like a neighbouring sound. In English, the prefix 'in-' becomes 'im-' before a labial consonant, so 'in-possible' is pronounced 'impossible', and /n/ becomes [ŋ] before /k/ in 'ink'. In practice this means working through real examples such as impossible, ink, input, ten bikes: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. import hashlib
inputs = ["impossible", "ink", "input", "ten bikes", "unfair", "incomplete"]
figures = {"assimilated_forms": 3, "place_changes": 2, "example_words": 6, "features_spread": 1}
def transform(x):
    return x.replace('n', 'm') if x.endswith(('p','b')) else x.replace('in', 'im') if x.startswith('in') else x
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Phonological Rules JAH-PHONO-000011 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Phonological rules are formal statements that derive surface pronunciations from underlying forms, such as the English plural rule that adds /s/ after voiceless sounds, /z/ after voiced sounds, and /ɪz/ after sibilants, giving 'cats', 'dogs', and 'horses'. Rules apply in order, can feed or bleed one another, and are stated with features so that one rule covers a whole class of sounds. In practice this means working through real examples such as cats, dogs, horses, books: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Rule-based phonology was the core of generative phonology in Chomsky and Halle's The Sound Pattern of English (1968), where ordered rules replaced the earlier taxonomic phoneme. Later theories kept rules in different forms, and rule ordering remains a standard explanation of opaque sound patterns. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Phonological rules are formal statements that derive surface pronunciations from underlying forms, such as the English plural rule that adds /s/ after voiceless sounds, /z/ after voiced sounds, and /ɪz/ after sibilants, giving 'cats', 'dogs', and 'horses'. Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["cats", "dogs", "horses", "books", "bags", "buses"]
figures = {"plural_allomorphs": 3, "example_words": 6, "voiceless_cases": 2, "sibilant_cases": 2}
def transform(x):
    return x + 'es' if x.endswith(('s','x','ch','sh')) else x + 's'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class PhonologicalRulesProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["cats", "dogs", "horses", "books", "bags", "buses"]
        figures = {"plural_allomorphs": 3, "example_words": 6, "voiceless_cases": 2, "sibilant_cases": 2}
        def transform(x):

        def compute(data):

            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)

            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")

Optimality Theory JAH-PHONO-000012 · complex

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Optimality theory holds that a language's grammar is a set of universal constraints ranked in a language-specific order, and the winning pronunciation is the candidate that violates the highest-ranked constraints least. A constraint against codas outranks faithfulness in Hawaiian, so loanwords lose their final consonants, while the reverse ranking in English keeps them. The theory explains typological patterns by constraint ranking rather than by language-specific rules. In practice this means working through real examples such as Hawaiian, English, Japanese, Spanish: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Optimality theory was proposed by Alan Prince and Paul Smolensky in 1993 and developed in the work of John McCarthy and others. It quickly became a dominant framework in phonology, applied to stress, syllable structure, tone, and morphology, and it introduced the tableau as its standard form of argument. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Optimality theory holds that a language's grammar is a set of universal constraints ranked in a language-specific order, and the winning pronunciation is the candidate that violates the highest-ranked constraints least. A constraint against codas outranks faithfulness in Hawaiian, so loanwords lose their final consonants, while the reverse ranking in English keeps them. The theory explains typological patterns by constraint ranking rather than by language-specific rules. In practice this means working through real examples such as Hawaiian, English, Japanese, Spanish: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. Vowel Harmony JAH-PHONO-000013 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Vowel harmony is the requirement that the vowels within a word agree in some feature, such as backness or rounding. In Turkish, the plural suffix is '-ler' after front vowels and '-lar' after back vowels: 'ev' (house, front vowel) takes '-ler' to give 'evler', while 'at' (horse, back vowel) takes '-lar' to give 'atlar'. Harmony spreads a feature across the word and is a hallmark of Turkic, Finno-Ugric, and many African languages. In practice this means working through real examples such as evler, atlar, guller, kizlar: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Vowel harmony was described by nineteenth-century Turkologists and Finno-Ugric scholars, and analysed in autosegmental phonology as the spreading of a vowel feature across a harmonic domain. Later work showed that harmony can be blocked by neutral vowels and that it interacts with stress and morphology. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Vowel harmony is the requirement that the vowels within a word agree in some feature, such as backness or rounding. In Turkish, the plural suffix is '-ler' after front vowels and '-lar' after back vowels: 'ev' (house, front vowel) takes '-ler' to give 'evler', while 'at' (horse, back vowel) takes '-lar' to give 'atlar'. Harmony spreads a feature across the word and is a hallmark of Turkic, Finno-Ugric, and many African languages. In practice this means working through real examples such as evler, atlar, guller, kizlar: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The explanation is supported by the linguistic evidence in the cited sources: attested examples, corpus data where applicable, and the descriptive tradition summarised in the history above. Vowel harmony is the requirement that the vowels within a word agree in some feature, such as backness or rounding. In Turkish, the plural suffix is '-ler' after front vowels and '-lar' after back vowels: 'ev' (house, front vowel) takes '-ler' to give 'evler', while 'at' (horse, back vowel) takes '-lar' to give 'atlar'. Harmony spreads a feature across the word and is a hallmark of Turkic, Finno-Ugric, and many African languages. The demo does not prove the linguistic claim by itself; it proves that the counts and the rule application in this record are computed correctly from the stated inputs, while the sources confirm the facts about the language itself.

Limits

This explanation does not apply everywhere. Vowel Harmony has edge cases and disputed analyses, and different schools describe it in different terms; the history above names the main traditions, and a reader should compare them. Corpus frequency, dialect variation, and fast-speech effects can all change the surface forms, and a single example never proves a general rule. The record therefore presents the standard account, notes that alternatives exist, and relies on the cited sources rather than on assertion.

History

Vowel harmony was described by nineteenth-century Turkologists and Finno-Ugric scholars, and analysed in autosegmental phonology as the spreading of a vowel feature across a harmonic domain. Later work showed that harmony can be blocked by neutral vowels and that it interacts with stress and morphology. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["evler", "atlar", "guller", "kizlar", "ev", "at"]
figures = {"harmonic_words": 4, "suffix_forms": 2, "example_words": 6, "features": 1}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class VowelHarmonyProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["evler", "atlar", "guller", "kizlar", "ev", "at"]
        figures = {"harmonic_words": 4, "suffix_forms": 2, "example_words": 6, "features": 1}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(VowelHarmonyProgram().run())

Nasalization JAH-PHONO-000014 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Nasalization is the process by which a vowel acquires nasality from a neighbouring nasal consonant, as in French 'bon' where the vowel is nasalised before the nasal, or in English where the vowel in 'man' is partly nasalised before /n/. In many languages nasal vowels are contrastive phonemes, so 'beau' and 'bon' differ by nasality alone. In practice this means working through real examples such as bon, beau, man, can: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Nasal vowels were described in the phonetics of French and Portuguese and studied acoustically in the twentieth century. Phonological theory treats nasalization as the spreading of the [+nasal] feature from a consonant to a vowel, and nasal harmony in some languages spreads nasality across whole words. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["bon", "beau", "man", "can", "sing", "bank"]
figures = {"nasal_vowels": 3, "example_words": 6, "nasal_consonants": 3, "languages": 2}
def transform(x):
    return x.replace('n', 'm') if x.endswith(('p','b')) else x.replace('in', 'im') if x.startswith('in') else x
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Lenition JAH-PHONO-000015 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Lenition is the weakening of a consonant, typically between vowels or at the end of a word, as when American English /t/ becomes a flap [ɾ] in 'water', or Spanish /d/ becomes a fricative between vowels. Lenition follows a strength hierarchy from stops through fricatives to approximants and zero, and it is a common historical sound change. In practice this means working through real examples such as water, butter, city, butterfly: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Lenition was recognised by nineteenth-century historical linguists tracing intervocalic weakening in Romance and Celtic languages. It was formalised as a strength scale by phonologists such as Theo Vennemann, and it remains a key process in explaining consonant alternations and historical change. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Lenition is the weakening of a consonant, typically between vowels or at the end of a word, as when American English /t/ becomes a flap [ɾ] in 'water', or Spanish /d/ becomes a fricative between vowels. Lenition follows a strength hierarchy from stops through fricatives to approximants and zero, and it is a common historical sound change. In practice this means working through real examples such as water, butter, city, butterfly: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["water", "butter", "city", "butterfly", "atom", "little"]
figures = {"flapped_words": 4, "example_words": 6, "positions": 2, "steps_on_scale": 3}
def transform(x):
    return x.replace('t', 'r') if 'a' in x else x
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class LenitionProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["water", "butter", "city", "butterfly", "atom", "little"]
        figures = {"flapped_words": 4, "example_words": 6, "positions": 2, "steps_on_scale": 3}
        def transform(x):
            return x.replace('t', 'r') if 'a' in x else x
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(LenitionProgram().run())

Metathesis JAH-PHONO-000016 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Metathesis is the swapping of two sounds in a word, as when Old English 'brid' became modern 'bird', or when some speakers say 'aks' for 'ask'. Metathesis can be sporadic in speech errors or regular in a language's history, and it often repairs a sequence that is hard to pronounce or that violates the language's phonotactics. In practice this means working through real examples such as brid, bird, aks, ask: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Metathesis was documented by historical linguists in the nineteenth century in the history of English, Romance, and Semitic languages. Formal phonology has treated it as a reordering operation, and psycholinguistic work shows it is a common slip of the tongue when sounds are similar. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Metathesis is the swapping of two sounds in a word, as when Old English 'brid' became modern 'bird', or when some speakers say 'aks' for 'ask'. Metathesis can be sporadic in speech errors or regular in a language's history, and it often repairs a sequence that is hard to pronounce or that violates the language's phonotactics. In practice this means working through real examples such as brid, bird, aks, ask: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The checkable counts are {'historical_cases': 2, 'example_words': 6, 'swapped_pairs': 2, 'languages': 3}, and the demo recomputes them from the example list, so a reader who runs the demo obtains the same numbers. Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["brid", "bird", "aks", "ask", "third", "thrid"]
figures = {"historical_cases": 2, "example_words": 6, "swapped_pairs": 2, "languages": 3}
def transform(x):
    return x[1] + x[0] + x[2:] if len(x) > 2 else x
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class MetathesisProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["brid", "bird", "aks", "ask", "third", "thrid"]
        figures = {"historical_cases": 2, "example_words": 6, "swapped_pairs": 2, "languages": 3}
        def transform(x):
            return x[1] + x[0] + x[2:] if len(x) > 2 else x
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(MetathesisProgram().run())
