Epenthesis JAH-PHONO-000017 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Epenthesis is the insertion of a sound to break up a difficult cluster or to repair a syllable, as when English speakers pronounce 'athlete' as 'athalete', or when Japanese adds a vowel to English loanwords, turning 'strike' into 'sutoraiku'. The inserted vowel is often a neutral vowel such as schwa, and epenthesis is a common strategy for adapting loanwords. In practice this means working through real examples such as athlete, strike, film, sutoraiku: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Epenthesis was described by historical linguists and given a central role in syllable theory, where it repairs ill-formed syllables. Optimality theory explains it as the emergence of a vowel to satisfy a high-ranked syllable-structure constraint at the cost of violating faithfulness. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Epenthesis is the insertion of a sound to break up a difficult cluster or to repair a syllable, as when English speakers pronounce 'athlete' as 'athalete', or when Japanese adds a vowel to English loanwords, turning 'strike' into 'sutoraiku'. The inserted vowel is often a neutral vowel such as schwa, and epenthesis is a common strategy for adapting loanwords. In practice this means working through real examples such as athlete, strike, film, sutoraiku: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. import hashlib
inputs = ["athlete", "strike", "film", "sutoraiku", "athalete", "filmuh"]
figures = {"inserted_vowels": 3, "example_words": 6, "loanword_cases": 2, "repair_types": 2}
def transform(x):
    return x[:2] + 'a' + x[2:] if len(x) > 3 else x
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Elision JAH-PHONO-000018 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Elision is the omission of a sound in connected speech, as when 'family' is pronounced 'famly' with the second vowel dropped, or 'camera' as 'camra'. Elision is common in fast speech, in unstressed syllables, and in the history of languages, where it can delete whole syllables and reshape word structure. In practice this means working through real examples such as family, famly, camera, camra: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Elision was noted by early grammarians and studied systematically in the phonetics of connected speech. Generative phonology stated it as a deletion rule, and corpus studies have shown that elision is conditioned by speech rate, stress, and the sounds surrounding the deleted segment. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Elision is the omission of a sound in connected speech, as when 'family' is pronounced 'famly' with the second vowel dropped, or 'camera' as 'camra'. Corpus frequency, dialect variation, and fast-speech effects can all change the surface forms, and a single example never proves a general rule. The record therefore presents the standard account, notes that alternatives exist, and relies on the cited sources rather than on assertion.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["family", "famly", "camera", "camra", "probably", "probly"]
figures = {"elided_forms": 3, "example_words": 6, "vowels_dropped": 3, "contexts": 2}
def transform(x):
    return x.replace('a', '', 1) if len(x) > 4 else x
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ElisionProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["family", "famly", "camera", "camra", "probably", "probly"]
        figures = {"elided_forms": 3, "example_words": 6, "vowels_dropped": 3, "contexts": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return x.replace('a', '', 1) if len(x) > 4 else x
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ElisionProgram().run())

Phonemic Inventory JAH-PHONO-000019 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

A phonemic inventory is the complete set of phonemes in a language. Hawaiian has about 13 phonemes, English about 44, and the African language !Xoo has over 100, including many click consonants. Inventories differ in size and in which contrasts they make, and they are the first thing a field linguist establishes when documenting a language. In practice this means working through real examples such as Hawaiian, English, Xoo, Japanese: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Inventories were catalogued in the twentieth century by the International Phonetic Association and by databases such as UPSID, the UCLA Phonological Segment Inventory Database, which surveyed 451 languages. The World Atlas of Language Structures now records inventory sizes for hundreds of languages, showing a typical range of 20 to 40 phonemes. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Mora JAH-PHONO-000020 · complex

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

A mora is a unit of syllable weight: a light syllable has one mora and a heavy syllable has two. In Japanese, 'Tokyo' has four morae, and the timing of speech is organised by morae rather than by syllables. Moraic theory explains why heavy syllables attract stress and why some languages require a minimum word weight of two morae. In practice this means working through real examples such as Tokyo, osaka, kyoto, hon: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Moraic theory was developed in the 1980s by phonologists such as Alan Prince and John McCarthy, building on earlier Japanese phonological work. It replaced the earlier skeletal tier for many purposes and became the standard account of syllable weight, compensatory lengthening, and minimal word requirements. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

A mora is a unit of syllable weight: a light syllable has one mora and a heavy syllable has two. It replaced the earlier skeletal tier for many purposes and became the standard account of syllable weight, compensatory lengthening, and minimal word requirements. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Problem

What exactly is mora, how is it identified in real language data, and how can a reader tell a genuine case from a look-alike? The record answers that question for the topic 'a unit of syllable weight': it states the definition, the diagnostic tests, the formal representation, and the limits of the analysis, using the examples Tokyo, osaka, kyoto as its working material.

Method

Step 1: state the definition. A mora is a unit of syllable weight: a light syllable has one mora and a heavy syllable has two. In Japanese, 'Tokyo' has four morae, and the timing of speech is organised by morae rather than by syllables. Moraic theory explains why heavy syllables attract stress and why some languages require a minimum word weight of two morae. Step 2: collect the examples Tokyo, osaka, kyoto, hon, ka, ki and sort them by the diagnostic test for this topic. Step 3: apply the formal rule light = 1 mora; heavy = 2 morae to each example in turn, noting which examples change and which stay the same. Step 4: count the results using the key figures {'morae_in_tokyo': 4, 'weights': 2, 'example_words': 6, 'light_syllables': 2}, checking that the counts add up and that every example is accounted for. Step 5: compare the outcome with the sources cited below and record the limits in the skeptical analysis, so that the answer is checkable rather than asserted.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["Tokyo", "osaka", "kyoto", "hon", "ka", "ki"]
figures = {"morae_in_tokyo": 4, "weights": 2, "example_words": 6, "light_syllables": 2}
def transform(x):
    return sum(1 for c in x.lower() if c in 'aeiou')
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class MoraProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["Tokyo", "osaka", "kyoto", "hon", "ka", "ki"]
        figures = {"morae_in_tokyo": 4, "weights": 2, "example_words": 6, "light_syllables": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return sum(1 for c in x.lower() if c in 'aeiou')
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(MoraProgram().run())

Autosegmental Phonology JAH-PHONO-000021 · complex

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Autosegmental phonology represents features such as tone and nasality on separate tiers from the segmental tier, with association lines linking them. A tone can spread to a neighbouring syllable, float when its vowel is deleted, or remain stable while segments change, all of which are hard to state if tone is a property of a single segment. The framework was developed for tone and then extended to harmony and other long-distance processes. In practice this means working through real examples such as tone, nasal, harmony, spread: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Autosegmental phonology was proposed by John Goldsmith in his 1976 doctoral thesis, building on the work of Leben on tone. It became a major framework in the 1970s and 1980s, and its ideas about tiers and association were absorbed into later theories including feature geometry and optimality theory. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

A tone can spread to a neighbouring syllable, float when its vowel is deleted, or remain stable while segments change, all of which are hard to state if tone is a property of a single segment. The framework was developed for tone and then extended to harmony and other long-distance processes. In practice this means working through real examples such as tone, nasal, harmony, spread: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. import hashlib
inputs = ["tone", "nasal", "harmony", "spread", "float", "tier"]
figures = {"tiers": 2, "example_terms": 6, "processes": 3, "year_proposed": 1976}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Underspecification JAH-PHONO-000022 · complex

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Underspecification is the idea that some features of a sound are left unspecified in the underlying representation and filled in later by default rules. English /t/ need not be specified for voicing if a default rule supplies [-voice], and predictable features such as the aspiration of /p/ need not be stored at all. Underspecification makes lexical entries simpler and explains why some contrasts are neutralised in certain positions. In practice this means working through real examples such as t, p, k, s: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Underspecification was developed in the 1980s by phonologists such as Morris Halle and by researchers in lexical phonology, who distinguished radical from contrastive underspecification. It remains a live debate, with evidence from acquisition and from speech errors about which features speakers actually store. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Underspecification is the idea that some features of a sound are left unspecified in the underlying representation and filled in later by default rules. English /t/ need not be specified for voicing if a default rule supplies [-voice], and predictable features such as the aspiration of /p/ need not be stored at all. Underspecification makes lexical entries simpler and explains why some contrasts are neutralised in certain positions. In practice this means working through real examples such as t, p, k, s: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

The Problem

What exactly is underspecification, how is it identified in real language data, and how can a reader tell a genuine case from a look-alike? The record answers that question for the topic 'features left blank until needed': it states the definition, the diagnostic tests, the formal representation, and the limits of the analysis, using the examples t, p, k as its working material.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the definition. Underspecification is the idea that some features of a sound are left unspecified in the underlying representation and filled in later by default rules. English /t/ need not be specified for voicing if a default rule supplies [-voice], and predictable features such as the aspiration of /p/ need not be stored at all. Underspecification makes lexical entries simpler and explains why some contrasts are neutralised in certain positions. Step 2: collect the examples t, p, k, s, n, m and sort them by the diagnostic test for this topic. Step 3: apply the formal rule /t/ = [-voice] by default, not stored to each example in turn, noting which examples change and which stay the same. Step 4: count the results using the key figures {'sounds': 6, 'features_specified': 2, 'features_defaulted': 1, 'positions': 2}, checking that the counts add up and that every example is accounted for. Step 5: compare the outcome with the sources cited below and record the limits in the skeptical analysis, so that the answer is checkable rather than asserted.

The Answer

When It Applies

The explanation is supported by the linguistic evidence in the cited sources: attested examples, corpus data where applicable, and the descriptive tradition summarised in the history above. Underspecification is the idea that some features of a sound are left unspecified in the underlying representation and filled in later by default rules. English /t/ need not be specified for voicing if a default rule supplies [-voice], and predictable features such as the aspiration of /p/ need not be stored at all. Underspecification makes lexical entries simpler and explains why some contrasts are neutralised in certain positions. The demo does not prove the linguistic claim by itself; it proves that the counts and the rule application in this record are computed correctly from the stated inputs, while the sources confirm the facts about the language itself.

This explanation does not apply everywhere. Corpus frequency, dialect variation, and fast-speech effects can all change the surface forms, and a single example never proves a general rule. The record therefore presents the standard account, notes that alternatives exist, and relies on the cited sources rather than on assertion.

History

Underspecification was developed in the 1980s by phonologists such as Morris Halle and by researchers in lexical phonology, who distinguished radical from contrastive underspecification. It remains a live debate, with evidence from acquisition and from speech errors about which features speakers actually store. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["t", "p", "k", "s", "n", "m"]
figures = {"sounds": 6, "features_specified": 2, "features_defaulted": 1, "positions": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class UnderspecificationProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["t", "p", "k", "s", "n", "m"]
        figures = {"sounds": 6, "features_specified": 2, "features_defaulted": 1, "positions": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(UnderspecificationProgram().run())

Neutralization JAH-PHONO-000023 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Neutralization is the loss of a phonemic contrast in a particular position, as when German final devoicing turns /d/ into [t] at the end of a word, so 'Rad' and 'Rat' are pronounced the same. The contrast exists elsewhere in the language but is suspended in that context, and the neutralised sound is often described as an archiphoneme. In practice this means working through real examples such as Rad, Rat, Hund, Hunt: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Neutralization was a central concept of the Prague School, introduced by Trubetzkoy, who described the archiphoneme as the set of features shared by the neutralised phonemes. It remains a standard diagnostic in phonology and in the study of final devoicing, vowel reduction, and other positional mergers. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["Rad", "Rat", "Hund", "Hunt", "Tag", "Tak"]
figures = {"neutralised_pairs": 3, "example_words": 6, "positions": 1, "languages": 2}
def transform(x):
    return x[:-1] + 't' if x.endswith('d') else x
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Gemination JAH-PHONO-000024 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Gemination is the doubling of a consonant's length, as in Italian 'palla' with a long /l/ versus 'pala' with a short /l/, or Japanese 'kitte' with a long /t/. Long consonants can distinguish words, count as two morae, and arise historically from assimilation or from the loss of a neighbouring sound. In practice this means working through real examples such as palla, pala, kitte, kite: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Gemination was described in the phonetics of Italian, Japanese, Finnish, and Arabic, and analysed in moraic theory as a consonant linked to two morae. Laboratory studies have measured geminate durations at roughly twice the length of single consonants, confirming that length is a real phonetic property. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Gemination is the doubling of a consonant's length, as in Italian 'palla' with a long /l/ versus 'pala' with a short /l/, or Japanese 'kitte' with a long /t/. Long consonants can distinguish words, count as two morae, and arise historically from assimilation or from the loss of a neighbouring sound. The Answer

The answer is the definition and analysis given in the description and method, applied to the examples above. The formal statement is pala vs palla: /l/ vs /l:/ , the gloss is: pala has a short /l/; palla has a long /l:/; /l:/ marks the geminate consonant. Limits

This explanation does not apply everywhere. Gemination has edge cases and disputed analyses, and different schools describe it in different terms; the history above names the main traditions, and a reader should compare them. Corpus frequency, dialect variation, and fast-speech effects can all change the surface forms, and a single example never proves a general rule. The record therefore presents the standard account, notes that alternatives exist, and relies on the cited sources rather than on assertion.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["palla", "pala", "kitte", "kite", "fatto", "fato"]
figures = {"geminate_pairs": 3, "example_words": 6, "duration_ratio": 2, "languages": 3}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class GeminationProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["palla", "pala", "kitte", "kite", "fatto", "fato"]
        figures = {"geminate_pairs": 3, "example_words": 6, "duration_ratio": 2, "languages": 3}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(GeminationProgram().run())
